I know those are supposed to be different things — a number, or a medical range, versus what I actually perceive — but in my head they get very tangled.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror before going to the lab, for example, I don’t usually register myself as thin. I don’t look and think, “Oh, that’s fine, that’s acceptable.” I mostly zoom in on certain areas — my stomach, or my hips — and they can look wrong to me, or too much, even if someone else might not see that at all. It’s almost like the rest of me disappears and those parts become the whole picture.\n\nAnd it affects how I feel about myself professionally too, which sounds strange saying it out loud. If my stomach or hips look noticeable to me, I start feeling like I look careless, or unserious, like I haven’t been disciplined enough. Especially before going into the lab, where I want to feel composed and competent. I know logically that someone’s body shape has nothing to do with how serious they are as a scientist, and I would never judge another person that way. But with myself, it feels different.\n\nSo if I were at what you called a healthy weight, I think I might still look in the mirror and see those parts as too big. I don’t think the feeling would just disappear because the number was medically okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape defines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I want it to. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow or irrational when I say it plainly, but it doesn’t feel shallow in the moment. It feels tied to whether I’m disciplined, whether I’m competent, whether I’m sort of… acceptable.\n\nLike before I go into the lab, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see myself as thin or okay. I immediately focus on my stomach or my hips, and if they look wrong to me, or too noticeable, it changes how I feel about myself for the whole day. It’s not just “I don’t like how I look.” It becomes, “I look careless,” or “I look unserious,” like my body is somehow giving people evidence that I’m not controlled enough or not put together enough.\n\nI know, logically, that my work and my value as a person shouldn’t have anything to do with my body shape. I would never think that about a colleague. But with myself, it’s very hard to separate them. If I feel like my body looks unacceptable, I feel unacceptable. So yes, I think my weight and shape end up carrying a lot of my self-worth, even if part of me knows they shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were on the verge of losing control while eating, even if you didn't actually lose control?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’ve had what you would call being “on the verge of losing control.” I don’t think I usually experience it as suddenly feeling like I’m going to eat everything or that I can’t stop myself, exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I can feel very anxious around eating, especially if the situation feels unplanned or if there’s more food than I expected, but I’m usually very aware of myself in those moments. Maybe too aware. I’m thinking about what’s there, what I’m doing, what other people might notice, whether I’ll regret it afterward. So it doesn’t feel like losing control so much as feeling afraid that the situation could get out of my control.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I worry, “If I start, will I be able to keep it contained?” But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as what you’re asking. I haven’t really thought of it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control over your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly, and I have a hard time separating them. The weight part is definitely there. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like something has slipped or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It doesn’t feel neutral to me, even if it’s a tiny change that I know could be normal. My mind immediately treats it like evidence that I’ve done something wrong.\n\nBut the reason it feels so frightening is also the control part. It’s not just the number itself — it’s what the number means to me. If it goes up, I feel like I’m not in control of my body, or not in control of myself. And that feels really intolerable. Like I’ve been holding everything together, and then suddenly there’s this sign that maybe I’m not as disciplined as I thought.\n\nSo if I had to answer directly, I’d say avoiding weight gain is a big part of it, but maintaining control is wrapped around that. The fear of gaining even a little makes me feel like I have to be stricter, because otherwise I’ll lose that sense that I’m managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision about what to eat more influenced by the fear of gaining weight or by the need to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think if I’m really honest, the fear of gaining weight is the thing that hits first. The control part is there too, but it’s almost like the fear of the number going up is what triggers the need to clamp down harder.\n\nLike, when I’m deciding what to eat, I’m thinking ahead to what it might do to my weight. Even if it’s something small or ordinary, I’ll start calculating it in my head and imagining how I’ll feel if the scale is different. If it goes up even a tiny amount — even something that probably shouldn’t matter — I feel this sharp panic. It’s not just disappointment. It feels like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain.\n\nAnd then I start judging myself through that. Like, if I choose the “wrong” thing, it means I’ve been careless, or weak, or I’ve let something slip. So the need for control is very tied into it, but I think the fear of gaining weight is what makes the decision feel so urgent and loaded. It’s hard for me to just see food as food. It becomes this decision that could either protect me from that panic or expose me to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that people say I am, and I know they’re worried. I’m not trying to pretend they haven’t said it. But I don’t really experience myself that way, if that makes sense.\n\nTo me, “underweight” sounds like something where your body is clearly failing or you can’t function, and I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, still doing my experiments, still keeping up with things. Maybe not perfectly, but I’m not just collapsed or unable to manage my life. So when people act like it’s medically dangerous, part of me thinks they’re overstating it.\n\nI can accept that other people may see me differently than I see myself, but I honestly don’t feel like I’m at some extreme or unsafe point. I feel like I’m managing. Maybe they’re concerned because I look different to them, or because they don’t understand how much I can still do, but from the inside it doesn’t feel as alarming as they make it sound.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… it’s hard for me to separate those, because the weight and the control feel tangled together. But if I’m being precise, the weight part is what I’m always watching for. The scale feels like this kind of verdict, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic.

If it goes up even a tiny amount — like, even a fraction — I get this sharp panic. It’s immediate. I don’t think, “Oh, bodies fluctuate,” or anything reasonable like that. I think, “I’ve let something slip.” Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. And then the routine becomes the way I try to get that feeling back under control.

So I suppose the routine is about achieving or protecting a certain weight, but not in a calm, goal-oriented way. It’s more that any movement upward feels threatening. And when that happens, I feel like I have to tighten everything again — the eating, the exercise, all of it — because otherwise it feels like I’m not managing myself properly.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… honestly, I’m here because occupational health sort of pushed the issue. I don’t know that I would have booked this myself, at least not right away. They’ve noticed, I suppose, that I’ve got into this pattern at work where I just keep putting off eating.\n\nI’m a biological scientist, so on lab days especially, or when there’s exhibition-related work going on, it’s very easy for me to say, “I’ll have breakfast after this run,” or “I’ll grab lunch once I’ve finished this bit,” and then one thing becomes another. By the time I actually do eat, I tend to keep it very small and… controlled, I guess. It feels easier that way, more manageable. So yes, that’s mostly why I’m here. Occupational health thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose I have. I don’t weigh myself constantly or anything, but the numbers have been drifting down when I do check, and my clothes are definitely looser. Things that used to fit properly now sort of hang differently, especially around the waist and shoulders.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows that’s probably what occupational health is concerned about, but another part of me sees it as… I don’t know, evidence that I’m keeping things contained. Like if work is chaotic and unpredictable, then at least I’m not letting that spill over into my body. It feels like being focused, or disciplined, rather than something I need to be alarmed about. But yes, if you’re asking directly, I have lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t always call it fear, because that sounds so dramatic, and I can usually explain it to myself as just wanting to stay in control or not undo things. But if I’m honest, if the scale goes up even a little bit — even a fraction — I get this sharp, horrible panic, like I’ve slipped somehow.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, that’s annoying.” It feels much bigger than that in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’re losing the discipline you worked so hard to maintain,” and then I feel this pressure to correct it, to get back to where I was. I know that might sound excessive when we’re talking about a tiny change, but it doesn’t feel tiny to me when I see it. It feels like evidence that I’m not managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean… “excessive” is a difficult word, isn’t it? Because what other people think is a normal amount can feel like quite a lot to me, especially if it’s something I hadn’t planned for or if it’s outside what I’d mentally allowed myself.\n\nBut in terms of eating a large amount in a short period, like suddenly having a lot of food all at once — I don’t think that’s really something I do. If anything, I tend to sort of pick at things or keep portions very contained. Sometimes if I’ve delayed eating for ages I might feel very aware that I’m hungry, but I still wouldn’t say I eat what I’d consider a big or uncontrolled amount. It’s more that I’m measuring it out in my head the whole time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in the way I think you mean. I don’t tend to have that sense of suddenly eating and not being able to stop, or coming round afterwards thinking, “What happened?” It’s almost the opposite, if anything. I’m very aware of what I’m doing while I’m eating — probably too aware, honestly — so it doesn’t feel out of control so much as very monitored.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I feel emotionally unsettled around food, especially if something wasn’t planned or if there’s more on a plate than I expected, but that’s not the same as losing control while eating. It’s more like I’m trying very hard to keep control, and the anxiety is about whether I’m managing that properly. So… no, I don’t think I’d say I lose control while eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit after eating. I’ve thought about why you’d ask that, and I understand it’s something you probably need to check, but it’s not something I do.\n\nI can feel very uncomfortable after eating, especially if I feel like I’ve had more than I meant to, but I don’t go and make myself sick. The distress is more in my head, if that makes sense — the thoughts and the guilt and the urge to mentally go over what I ate — but vomiting isn’t part of it for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to punish yourself for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I do exercise, but I don’t always think of it in such a direct way as, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” It’s more… it can get tied up with needing to feel like I’ve done something, if that makes sense.\n\nI wouldn’t say I go out thinking, “I’m punishing myself,” exactly. That sounds very deliberate and harsh when you put it like that. But I do notice that if I’ve eaten something that felt like more than I expected, or if I feel uneasy about it, I can feel more restless and like I should move around rather than sit still. It’s not always a formal workout or anything dramatic — sometimes it’s just feeling like I need to be active, or walking more, or not letting myself relax straight away.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it punishment. I don’t use that word in my head. But there is sometimes a sense that exercise or movement makes me feel a bit less guilty or less panicky about having eaten. That’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you've lost?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think I would be upset. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not even just a large amount of weight, honestly. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp, immediate panic, like something has slipped, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. I know that probably sounds disproportionate, but in the moment it feels very real. My mind goes straight to, “This is the start of it,” or “You’re letting things get away from you,” even if rationally I know bodies fluctuate and scales aren’t perfectly precise.\n\nI think gaining back the weight would feel like failure, or like all the effort and control I’ve put in had been undone. I don’t like saying that out loud, because part of me knows it sounds rigid, but that is honestly how it feels. It’s not neutral for me at all. It feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating with other people because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, although I don’t always frame it to myself as “I’m afraid of gaining weight” in that exact moment. It’s more that eating with other people feels unpredictable, and that makes me anxious.\n\nAt work, for example, I’m still functioning in the main ways. I’m meeting the important deadlines, I’m getting the necessary things done, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches, and I avoid those informal meals where people discuss gallery planning or just talk things through over food. I used to go to those, and now I find reasons not to.\n\nPart of it is that I don’t want people watching what I’m eating, or commenting, or the situation getting out of my control. And yes, underneath that, there’s the fear that if I eat in those settings I’ll have more than I’m comfortable with, or I’ll feel like it will affect my weight. So I end up staying away.\n\nIt’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. I don’t think I fully realized how much of the informal connection at work happens around meals until I stopped going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least, I don’t think “healthy weight” would automatically make me see myself as healthy-looking, if that makes sense. I know those are supposed to be different things — a number, or a medical range, versus what I actually perceive — but in my head they get very tangled.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror before going to the lab, for example, I don’t usually register myself as thin. I don’t look and think, “Oh, that’s fine, that’s acceptable.” I mostly zoom in on certain areas — my stomach, or my hips — and they can look wrong to me, or too much, even if someone else might not see that at all. It’s almost like the rest of me disappears and those parts become the whole picture.\n\nAnd it affects how I feel about myself professionally too, which sounds strange saying it out loud. If my stomach or hips look noticeable to me, I start feeling like I look careless, or unserious, like I haven’t been disciplined enough. Especially before going into the lab, where I want to feel composed and competent. I know logically that someone’s body shape has nothing to do with how serious they are as a scientist, and I would never judge another person that way. But with myself, it feels different.\n\nSo if I were at what you called a healthy weight, I think I might still look in the mirror and see those parts as too big. I don’t think the feeling would just disappear because the number was medically okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape defines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I want it to. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow or irrational when I say it plainly, but it doesn’t feel shallow in the moment. It feels tied to whether I’m disciplined, whether I’m competent, whether I’m sort of… acceptable.\n\nLike before I go into the lab, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see myself as thin or okay. I immediately focus on my stomach or my hips, and if they look wrong to me, or too noticeable, it changes how I feel about myself for the whole day. It’s not just “I don’t like how I look.” It becomes, “I look careless,” or “I look unserious,” like my body is somehow giving people evidence that I’m not controlled enough or not put together enough.\n\nI know, logically, that my work and my value as a person shouldn’t have anything to do with my body shape. I would never think that about a colleague. But with myself, it’s very hard to separate them. If I feel like my body looks unacceptable, I feel unacceptable. So yes, I think my weight and shape end up carrying a lot of my self-worth, even if part of me knows they shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were on the verge of losing control while eating, even if you didn't actually lose control?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’ve had what you would call being “on the verge of losing control.” I don’t think I usually experience it as suddenly feeling like I’m going to eat everything or that I can’t stop myself, exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I can feel very anxious around eating, especially if the situation feels unplanned or if there’s more food than I expected, but I’m usually very aware of myself in those moments. Maybe too aware. I’m thinking about what’s there, what I’m doing, what other people might notice, whether I’ll regret it afterward. So it doesn’t feel like losing control so much as feeling afraid that the situation could get out of my control.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I worry, “If I start, will I be able to keep it contained?” But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as what you’re asking. I haven’t really thought of it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control over your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly, and I have a hard time separating them. The weight part is definitely there. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like something has slipped or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It doesn’t feel neutral to me, even if it’s a tiny change that I know could be normal. My mind immediately treats it like evidence that I’ve done something wrong.\n\nBut the reason it feels so frightening is also the control part. It’s not just the number itself — it’s what the number means to me. If it goes up, I feel like I’m not in control of my body, or not in control of myself. And that feels really intolerable. Like I’ve been holding everything together, and then suddenly there’s this sign that maybe I’m not as disciplined as I thought.\n\nSo if I had to answer directly, I’d say avoiding weight gain is a big part of it, but maintaining control is wrapped around that. The fear of gaining even a little makes me feel like I have to be stricter, because otherwise I’ll lose that sense that I’m managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision about what to eat more influenced by the fear of gaining weight or by the need to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think if I’m really honest, the fear of gaining weight is the thing that hits first. The control part is there too, but it’s almost like the fear of the number going up is what triggers the need to clamp down harder.\n\nLike, when I’m deciding what to eat, I’m thinking ahead to what it might do to my weight. Even if it’s something small or ordinary, I’ll start calculating it in my head and imagining how I’ll feel if the scale is different. If it goes up even a tiny amount — even something that probably shouldn’t matter — I feel this sharp panic. It’s not just disappointment. It feels like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain.\n\nAnd then I start judging myself through that. Like, if I choose the “wrong” thing, it means I’ve been careless, or weak, or I’ve let something slip. So the need for control is very tied into it, but I think the fear of gaining weight is what makes the decision feel so urgent and loaded. It’s hard for me to just see food as food. It becomes this decision that could either protect me from that panic or expose me to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that people say I am, and I know they’re worried. I’m not trying to pretend they haven’t said it. But I don’t really experience myself that way, if that makes sense.\n\nTo me, “underweight” sounds like something where your body is clearly failing or you can’t function, and I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, still doing my experiments, still keeping up with things. Maybe not perfectly, but I’m not just collapsed or unable to manage my life. So when people act like it’s medically dangerous, part of me thinks they’re overstating it.\n\nI can accept that other people may see me differently than I see myself, but I honestly don’t feel like I’m at some extreme or unsafe point. I feel like I’m managing. Maybe they’re concerned because I look different to them, or because they don’t understand how much I can still do, but from the inside it doesn’t feel as alarming as they make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict eating and exercise routine more about achieving a certain weight or more about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s hard for me to separate those, because the weight and the control feel tangled together. But if I’m being precise, the weight part is what I’m always watching for. The scale feels like this kind of verdict, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic.\n\nIf it goes up even a tiny amount — like, even a fraction — I get this sharp panic. It’s immediate. I don’t think, “Oh, bodies fluctuate,” or anything reasonable like that. I think, “I’ve let something slip.” Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. And then the routine becomes the way I try to get that feeling back under control.\n\nSo I suppose the routine is about achieving or protecting a certain weight, but not in a calm, goal-oriented way. It’s more that any movement upward feels threatening. And when that happens, I feel like I have to tighten everything again — the eating, the exercise, all of it — because otherwise it feels like I’m not managing myself properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It becomes this decision that could either protect me from that panic or expose me to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that people say I am, and I know they’re worried. I’m not trying to pretend they haven’t said it. But I don’t really experience myself that way, if that makes sense.\n\nTo me, “underweight” sounds like something where your body is clearly failing or you can’t function, and I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, still doing my experiments, still keeping up with things. Maybe not perfectly, but I’m not just collapsed or unable to manage my life. So when people act like it’s medically dangerous, part of me thinks they’re overstating it.\n\nI can accept that other people may see me differently than I see myself, but I honestly don’t feel like I’m at some extreme or unsafe point. I feel like I’m managing. Maybe they’re concerned because I look different to them, or because they don’t understand how much I can still do, but from the inside it doesn’t feel as alarming as they make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict eating and exercise routine more about achieving a certain weight or more about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s hard for me to separate those, because the weight and the control feel tangled together. But if I’m being precise, the weight part is what I’m always watching for. The scale feels like this kind of verdict, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic.\n\nIf it goes up even a tiny amount — like, even a fraction — I get this sharp panic. It’s immediate. I don’t think, “Oh, bodies fluctuate,” or anything reasonable like that. I think, “I’ve let something slip.” Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. And then the routine becomes the way I try to get that feeling back under control.\n\nSo I suppose the routine is about achieving or protecting a certain weight, but not in a calm, goal-oriented way. It’s more that any movement upward feels threatening. And when that happens, I feel like I have to tighten everything again — the eating, the exercise, all of it — because otherwise it feels like I’m not managing myself properly.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s mostly to avoid gaining weight, if I’m being completely honest. The control part is there, but it almost comes after. Like… the fear happens first, and then the controlling behavior is how I try to deal with the fear.

If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It’s not just, “Oh, I gained a little,” or something neutral. It feels much bigger than that in my head. It feels like evidence that I’ve been careless, or that I’m slipping, or that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing.

So when I restrict, it’s not really because I’m calmly trying to organize my life or feel generally in control. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent that panic from happening. I’m trying to keep the number from going up, because if it does, even slightly, I immediately feel like I have to correct it somehow. The control is wrapped into it, but the main goal is avoiding the weight gain.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… honestly, I’m here because occupational health sort of pushed the issue. I don’t know that I would have booked this myself, at least not right away. They’ve noticed, I suppose, that I’ve got into this pattern at work where I just keep putting off eating.\n\nI’m a biological scientist, so on lab days especially, or when there’s exhibition-related work going on, it’s very easy for me to say, “I’ll have breakfast after this run,” or “I’ll grab lunch once I’ve finished this bit,” and then one thing becomes another. By the time I actually do eat, I tend to keep it very small and… controlled, I guess. It feels easier that way, more manageable. So yes, that’s mostly why I’m here. Occupational health thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose I have. I don’t weigh myself constantly or anything, but the numbers have been drifting down when I do check, and my clothes are definitely looser. Things that used to fit properly now sort of hang differently, especially around the waist and shoulders.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows that’s probably what occupational health is concerned about, but another part of me sees it as… I don’t know, evidence that I’m keeping things contained. Like if work is chaotic and unpredictable, then at least I’m not letting that spill over into my body. It feels like being focused, or disciplined, rather than something I need to be alarmed about. But yes, if you’re asking directly, I have lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t always call it fear, because that sounds so dramatic, and I can usually explain it to myself as just wanting to stay in control or not undo things. But if I’m honest, if the scale goes up even a little bit — even a fraction — I get this sharp, horrible panic, like I’ve slipped somehow.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, that’s annoying.” It feels much bigger than that in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’re losing the discipline you worked so hard to maintain,” and then I feel this pressure to correct it, to get back to where I was. I know that might sound excessive when we’re talking about a tiny change, but it doesn’t feel tiny to me when I see it. It feels like evidence that I’m not managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean… “excessive” is a difficult word, isn’t it? Because what other people think is a normal amount can feel like quite a lot to me, especially if it’s something I hadn’t planned for or if it’s outside what I’d mentally allowed myself.\n\nBut in terms of eating a large amount in a short period, like suddenly having a lot of food all at once — I don’t think that’s really something I do. If anything, I tend to sort of pick at things or keep portions very contained. Sometimes if I’ve delayed eating for ages I might feel very aware that I’m hungry, but I still wouldn’t say I eat what I’d consider a big or uncontrolled amount. It’s more that I’m measuring it out in my head the whole time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in the way I think you mean. I don’t tend to have that sense of suddenly eating and not being able to stop, or coming round afterwards thinking, “What happened?” It’s almost the opposite, if anything. I’m very aware of what I’m doing while I’m eating — probably too aware, honestly — so it doesn’t feel out of control so much as very monitored.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I feel emotionally unsettled around food, especially if something wasn’t planned or if there’s more on a plate than I expected, but that’s not the same as losing control while eating. It’s more like I’m trying very hard to keep control, and the anxiety is about whether I’m managing that properly. So… no, I don’t think I’d say I lose control while eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit after eating. I’ve thought about why you’d ask that, and I understand it’s something you probably need to check, but it’s not something I do.\n\nI can feel very uncomfortable after eating, especially if I feel like I’ve had more than I meant to, but I don’t go and make myself sick. The distress is more in my head, if that makes sense — the thoughts and the guilt and the urge to mentally go over what I ate — but vomiting isn’t part of it for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to punish yourself for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I do exercise, but I don’t always think of it in such a direct way as, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” It’s more… it can get tied up with needing to feel like I’ve done something, if that makes sense.\n\nI wouldn’t say I go out thinking, “I’m punishing myself,” exactly. That sounds very deliberate and harsh when you put it like that. But I do notice that if I’ve eaten something that felt like more than I expected, or if I feel uneasy about it, I can feel more restless and like I should move around rather than sit still. It’s not always a formal workout or anything dramatic — sometimes it’s just feeling like I need to be active, or walking more, or not letting myself relax straight away.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it punishment. I don’t use that word in my head. But there is sometimes a sense that exercise or movement makes me feel a bit less guilty or less panicky about having eaten. That’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you've lost?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think I would be upset. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not even just a large amount of weight, honestly. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp, immediate panic, like something has slipped, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. I know that probably sounds disproportionate, but in the moment it feels very real. My mind goes straight to, “This is the start of it,” or “You’re letting things get away from you,” even if rationally I know bodies fluctuate and scales aren’t perfectly precise.\n\nI think gaining back the weight would feel like failure, or like all the effort and control I’ve put in had been undone. I don’t like saying that out loud, because part of me knows it sounds rigid, but that is honestly how it feels. It’s not neutral for me at all. It feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating with other people because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, although I don’t always frame it to myself as “I’m afraid of gaining weight” in that exact moment. It’s more that eating with other people feels unpredictable, and that makes me anxious.\n\nAt work, for example, I’m still functioning in the main ways. I’m meeting the important deadlines, I’m getting the necessary things done, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches, and I avoid those informal meals where people discuss gallery planning or just talk things through over food. I used to go to those, and now I find reasons not to.\n\nPart of it is that I don’t want people watching what I’m eating, or commenting, or the situation getting out of my control. And yes, underneath that, there’s the fear that if I eat in those settings I’ll have more than I’m comfortable with, or I’ll feel like it will affect my weight. So I end up staying away.\n\nIt’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. I don’t think I fully realized how much of the informal connection at work happens around meals until I stopped going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least, I don’t think “healthy weight” would automatically make me see myself as healthy-looking, if that makes sense. I know those are supposed to be different things — a number, or a medical range, versus what I actually perceive — but in my head they get very tangled.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror before going to the lab, for example, I don’t usually register myself as thin. I don’t look and think, “Oh, that’s fine, that’s acceptable.” I mostly zoom in on certain areas — my stomach, or my hips — and they can look wrong to me, or too much, even if someone else might not see that at all. It’s almost like the rest of me disappears and those parts become the whole picture.\n\nAnd it affects how I feel about myself professionally too, which sounds strange saying it out loud. If my stomach or hips look noticeable to me, I start feeling like I look careless, or unserious, like I haven’t been disciplined enough. Especially before going into the lab, where I want to feel composed and competent. I know logically that someone’s body shape has nothing to do with how serious they are as a scientist, and I would never judge another person that way. But with myself, it feels different.\n\nSo if I were at what you called a healthy weight, I think I might still look in the mirror and see those parts as too big. I don’t think the feeling would just disappear because the number was medically okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape defines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I want it to. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow or irrational when I say it plainly, but it doesn’t feel shallow in the moment. It feels tied to whether I’m disciplined, whether I’m competent, whether I’m sort of… acceptable.\n\nLike before I go into the lab, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see myself as thin or okay. I immediately focus on my stomach or my hips, and if they look wrong to me, or too noticeable, it changes how I feel about myself for the whole day. It’s not just “I don’t like how I look.” It becomes, “I look careless,” or “I look unserious,” like my body is somehow giving people evidence that I’m not controlled enough or not put together enough.\n\nI know, logically, that my work and my value as a person shouldn’t have anything to do with my body shape. I would never think that about a colleague. But with myself, it’s very hard to separate them. If I feel like my body looks unacceptable, I feel unacceptable. So yes, I think my weight and shape end up carrying a lot of my self-worth, even if part of me knows they shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were on the verge of losing control while eating, even if you didn't actually lose control?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’ve had what you would call being “on the verge of losing control.” I don’t think I usually experience it as suddenly feeling like I’m going to eat everything or that I can’t stop myself, exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I can feel very anxious around eating, especially if the situation feels unplanned or if there’s more food than I expected, but I’m usually very aware of myself in those moments. Maybe too aware. I’m thinking about what’s there, what I’m doing, what other people might notice, whether I’ll regret it afterward. So it doesn’t feel like losing control so much as feeling afraid that the situation could get out of my control.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I worry, “If I start, will I be able to keep it contained?” But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as what you’re asking. I haven’t really thought of it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control over your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly, and I have a hard time separating them. The weight part is definitely there. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like something has slipped or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It doesn’t feel neutral to me, even if it’s a tiny change that I know could be normal. My mind immediately treats it like evidence that I’ve done something wrong.\n\nBut the reason it feels so frightening is also the control part. It’s not just the number itself — it’s what the number means to me. If it goes up, I feel like I’m not in control of my body, or not in control of myself. And that feels really intolerable. Like I’ve been holding everything together, and then suddenly there’s this sign that maybe I’m not as disciplined as I thought.\n\nSo if I had to answer directly, I’d say avoiding weight gain is a big part of it, but maintaining control is wrapped around that. The fear of gaining even a little makes me feel like I have to be stricter, because otherwise I’ll lose that sense that I’m managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision about what to eat more influenced by the fear of gaining weight or by the need to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think if I’m really honest, the fear of gaining weight is the thing that hits first. The control part is there too, but it’s almost like the fear of the number going up is what triggers the need to clamp down harder.\n\nLike, when I’m deciding what to eat, I’m thinking ahead to what it might do to my weight. Even if it’s something small or ordinary, I’ll start calculating it in my head and imagining how I’ll feel if the scale is different. If it goes up even a tiny amount — even something that probably shouldn’t matter — I feel this sharp panic. It’s not just disappointment. It feels like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain.\n\nAnd then I start judging myself through that. Like, if I choose the “wrong” thing, it means I’ve been careless, or weak, or I’ve let something slip. So the need for control is very tied into it, but I think the fear of gaining weight is what makes the decision feel so urgent and loaded. It’s hard for me to just see food as food. It becomes this decision that could either protect me from that panic or expose me to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that people say I am, and I know they’re worried. I’m not trying to pretend they haven’t said it. But I don’t really experience myself that way, if that makes sense.\n\nTo me, “underweight” sounds like something where your body is clearly failing or you can’t function, and I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, still doing my experiments, still keeping up with things. Maybe not perfectly, but I’m not just collapsed or unable to manage my life. So when people act like it’s medically dangerous, part of me thinks they’re overstating it.\n\nI can accept that other people may see me differently than I see myself, but I honestly don’t feel like I’m at some extreme or unsafe point. I feel like I’m managing. Maybe they’re concerned because I look different to them, or because they don’t understand how much I can still do, but from the inside it doesn’t feel as alarming as they make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict eating and exercise routine more about achieving a certain weight or more about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s hard for me to separate those, because the weight and the control feel tangled together. The scale feels like this kind of verdict, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic.\n\nIf it goes up even a tiny amount — like, even a fraction — I get this sharp panic. It’s immediate. I don’t think, “Oh, bodies fluctuate,” or anything reasonable like that. I think, “I’ve let something slip.” Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. And then the routine becomes the way I try to get that feeling back under control.\n\nSo I suppose the routine is about achieving or protecting a certain weight, but not in a calm, goal-oriented way. It’s more that any movement upward feels threatening. And when that happens, I feel like I have to tighten everything again — the eating, the exercise, all of it — because otherwise it feels like I’m not managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly to avoid gaining weight, if I’m being completely honest. Like… the fear happens first, and then the controlling behavior is how I try to deal with the fear.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It feels like evidence that I’ve been careless, or that I’m slipping, or that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nSo when I restrict, it’s not really because I’m calmly trying to organize my life or feel generally in control. I don’t know that I would have booked this myself, at least not right away. They’ve noticed, I suppose, that I’ve got into this pattern at work where I just keep putting off eating.\n\nI’m a biological scientist, so on lab days especially, or when there’s exhibition-related work going on, it’s very easy for me to say, “I’ll have breakfast after this run,” or “I’ll grab lunch once I’ve finished this bit,” and then one thing becomes another. By the time I actually do eat, I tend to keep it very small and… controlled, I guess. It feels easier that way, more manageable. So yes, that’s mostly why I’m here. Occupational health thought I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose I have. I don’t weigh myself constantly or anything, but the numbers have been drifting down when I do check, and my clothes are definitely looser. Things that used to fit properly now sort of hang differently, especially around the waist and shoulders.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because part of me knows that’s probably what occupational health is concerned about, but another part of me sees it as… I don’t know, evidence that I’m keeping things contained. Like if work is chaotic and unpredictable, then at least I’m not letting that spill over into my body. It feels like being focused, or disciplined, rather than something I need to be alarmed about. But yes, if you’re asking directly, I have lost weight.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t always call it fear, because that sounds so dramatic, and I can usually explain it to myself as just wanting to stay in control or not undo things. But if I’m honest, if the scale goes up even a little bit — even a fraction — I get this sharp, horrible panic, like I’ve slipped somehow.\n\nIt’s not just, “Oh, that’s annoying.” It feels much bigger than that in the moment. My mind goes straight to, “You’re losing the discipline you worked so hard to maintain,” and then I feel this pressure to correct it, to get back to where I was. I know that might sound excessive when we’re talking about a tiny change, but it doesn’t feel tiny to me when I see it. It feels like evidence that I’m not managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean… “excessive” is a difficult word, isn’t it? Because what other people think is a normal amount can feel like quite a lot to me, especially if it’s something I hadn’t planned for or if it’s outside what I’d mentally allowed myself.\n\nBut in terms of eating a large amount in a short period, like suddenly having a lot of food all at once — I don’t think that’s really something I do. If anything, I tend to sort of pick at things or keep portions very contained. Sometimes if I’ve delayed eating for ages I might feel very aware that I’m hungry, but I still wouldn’t say I eat what I’d consider a big or uncontrolled amount. It’s more that I’m measuring it out in my head the whole time, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in the way I think you mean. I don’t tend to have that sense of suddenly eating and not being able to stop, or coming round afterwards thinking, “What happened?” It’s almost the opposite, if anything. I’m very aware of what I’m doing while I’m eating — probably too aware, honestly — so it doesn’t feel out of control so much as very monitored.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I feel emotionally unsettled around food, especially if something wasn’t planned or if there’s more on a plate than I expected, but that’s not the same as losing control while eating. It’s more like I’m trying very hard to keep control, and the anxiety is about whether I’m managing that properly. So… no, I don’t think I’d say I lose control while eating.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit after eating. I’ve thought about why you’d ask that, and I understand it’s something you probably need to check, but it’s not something I do.\n\nI can feel very uncomfortable after eating, especially if I feel like I’ve had more than I meant to, but I don’t go and make myself sick. The distress is more in my head, if that makes sense — the thoughts and the guilt and the urge to mentally go over what I ate — but vomiting isn’t part of it for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise to burn off calories or to punish yourself for eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I do exercise, but I don’t always think of it in such a direct way as, “I ate this, so now I have to burn it off.” It’s more… it can get tied up with needing to feel like I’ve done something, if that makes sense.\n\nI wouldn’t say I go out thinking, “I’m punishing myself,” exactly. That sounds very deliberate and harsh when you put it like that. But I do notice that if I’ve eaten something that felt like more than I expected, or if I feel uneasy about it, I can feel more restless and like I should move around rather than sit still. It’s not always a formal workout or anything dramatic — sometimes it’s just feeling like I need to be active, or walking more, or not letting myself relax straight away.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it punishment. I don’t use that word in my head. But there is sometimes a sense that exercise or movement makes me feel a bit less guilty or less panicky about having eaten. That’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be upset if you gained back the weight you've lost?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think I would be upset. Probably more than I want to admit.\n\nIt’s not even just a large amount of weight, honestly. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this very sharp, immediate panic, like something has slipped, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. I know that probably sounds disproportionate, but in the moment it feels very real. My mind goes straight to, “This is the start of it,” or “You’re letting things get away from you,” even if rationally I know bodies fluctuate and scales aren’t perfectly precise.\n\nI think gaining back the weight would feel like failure, or like all the effort and control I’ve put in had been undone. I don’t like saying that out loud, because part of me knows it sounds rigid, but that is honestly how it feels. It’s not neutral for me at all. It feels frightening.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating with other people because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, although I don’t always frame it to myself as “I’m afraid of gaining weight” in that exact moment. It’s more that eating with other people feels unpredictable, and that makes me anxious.\n\nAt work, for example, I’m still functioning in the main ways. I’m meeting the important deadlines, I’m getting the necessary things done, so from the outside I probably look like I’m managing. But I’ve stopped going to lab lunches, and I avoid those informal meals where people discuss gallery planning or just talk things through over food. I used to go to those, and now I find reasons not to.\n\nPart of it is that I don’t want people watching what I’m eating, or commenting, or the situation getting out of my control. And yes, underneath that, there’s the fear that if I eat in those settings I’ll have more than I’m comfortable with, or I’ll feel like it will affect my weight. So I end up staying away.\n\nIt’s made me more isolated from colleagues than I used to be. I don’t think I fully realized how much of the informal connection at work happens around meals until I stopped going.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would see yourself as fat even if you were at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Or at least, I don’t think “healthy weight” would automatically make me see myself as healthy-looking, if that makes sense. I know those are supposed to be different things — a number, or a medical range, versus what I actually perceive — but in my head they get very tangled.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror before going to the lab, for example, I don’t usually register myself as thin. I don’t look and think, “Oh, that’s fine, that’s acceptable.” I mostly zoom in on certain areas — my stomach, or my hips — and they can look wrong to me, or too much, even if someone else might not see that at all. It’s almost like the rest of me disappears and those parts become the whole picture.\n\nAnd it affects how I feel about myself professionally too, which sounds strange saying it out loud. If my stomach or hips look noticeable to me, I start feeling like I look careless, or unserious, like I haven’t been disciplined enough. Especially before going into the lab, where I want to feel composed and competent. I know logically that someone’s body shape has nothing to do with how serious they are as a scientist, and I would never judge another person that way. But with myself, it feels different.\n\nSo if I were at what you called a healthy weight, I think I might still look in the mirror and see those parts as too big. I don’t think the feeling would just disappear because the number was medically okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape defines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I want it to. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so shallow or irrational when I say it plainly, but it doesn’t feel shallow in the moment. It feels tied to whether I’m disciplined, whether I’m competent, whether I’m sort of… acceptable.\n\nLike before I go into the lab, I’ll look in the mirror and I don’t really see myself as thin or okay. I immediately focus on my stomach or my hips, and if they look wrong to me, or too noticeable, it changes how I feel about myself for the whole day. It’s not just “I don’t like how I look.” It becomes, “I look careless,” or “I look unserious,” like my body is somehow giving people evidence that I’m not controlled enough or not put together enough.\n\nI know, logically, that my work and my value as a person shouldn’t have anything to do with my body shape. I would never think that about a colleague. But with myself, it’s very hard to separate them. If I feel like my body looks unacceptable, I feel unacceptable. So yes, I think my weight and shape end up carrying a lot of my self-worth, even if part of me knows they shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were on the verge of losing control while eating, even if you didn't actually lose control?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if I’ve had what you would call being “on the verge of losing control.” I don’t think I usually experience it as suddenly feeling like I’m going to eat everything or that I can’t stop myself, exactly.\n\nIt’s more… I can feel very anxious around eating, especially if the situation feels unplanned or if there’s more food than I expected, but I’m usually very aware of myself in those moments. Maybe too aware. I’m thinking about what’s there, what I’m doing, what other people might notice, whether I’ll regret it afterward. So it doesn’t feel like losing control so much as feeling afraid that the situation could get out of my control.\n\nI suppose there are moments where I worry, “If I start, will I be able to keep it contained?” But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as what you’re asking. I haven’t really thought of it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about maintaining a sense of control over your body?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly, and I have a hard time separating them. The weight part is definitely there. If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like something has slipped or I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It doesn’t feel neutral to me, even if it’s a tiny change that I know could be normal. My mind immediately treats it like evidence that I’ve done something wrong.\n\nBut the reason it feels so frightening is also the control part. It’s not just the number itself — it’s what the number means to me. If it goes up, I feel like I’m not in control of my body, or not in control of myself. And that feels really intolerable. Like I’ve been holding everything together, and then suddenly there’s this sign that maybe I’m not as disciplined as I thought.\n\nSo if I had to answer directly, I’d say avoiding weight gain is a big part of it, but maintaining control is wrapped around that. The fear of gaining even a little makes me feel like I have to be stricter, because otherwise I’ll lose that sense that I’m managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision about what to eat more influenced by the fear of gaining weight or by the need to feel in control of your body?\n\nPatient: I think if I’m really honest, the fear of gaining weight is the thing that hits first. The control part is there too, but it’s almost like the fear of the number going up is what triggers the need to clamp down harder.\n\nLike, when I’m deciding what to eat, I’m thinking ahead to what it might do to my weight. Even if it’s something small or ordinary, I’ll start calculating it in my head and imagining how I’ll feel if the scale is different. If it goes up even a tiny amount — even something that probably shouldn’t matter — I feel this sharp panic. It’s not just disappointment. It feels like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain.\n\nAnd then I start judging myself through that. Like, if I choose the “wrong” thing, it means I’ve been careless, or weak, or I’ve let something slip. So the need for control is very tied into it, but I think the fear of gaining weight is what makes the decision feel so urgent and loaded. It’s hard for me to just see food as food. It becomes this decision that could either protect me from that panic or expose me to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know that people say I am, and I know they’re worried. I’m not trying to pretend they haven’t said it. But I don’t really experience myself that way, if that makes sense.\n\nTo me, “underweight” sounds like something where your body is clearly failing or you can’t function, and I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, still doing my experiments, still keeping up with things. Maybe not perfectly, but I’m not just collapsed or unable to manage my life. So when people act like it’s medically dangerous, part of me thinks they’re overstating it.\n\nI can accept that other people may see me differently than I see myself, but I honestly don’t feel like I’m at some extreme or unsafe point. I feel like I’m managing. Maybe they’re concerned because I look different to them, or because they don’t understand how much I can still do, but from the inside it doesn’t feel as alarming as they make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Is your strict eating and exercise routine more about achieving a certain weight or more about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s hard for me to separate those, because the weight and the control feel tangled together. But if I’m being precise, the weight part is what I’m always watching for. The scale feels like this kind of verdict, even though I know that probably sounds dramatic.\n\nIf it goes up even a tiny amount — like, even a fraction — I get this sharp panic. It’s immediate. I don’t think, “Oh, bodies fluctuate,” or anything reasonable like that. I think, “I’ve let something slip.” Like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. And then the routine becomes the way I try to get that feeling back under control.\n\nSo I suppose the routine is about achieving or protecting a certain weight, but not in a calm, goal-oriented way. It’s more that any movement upward feels threatening. And when that happens, I feel like I have to tighten everything again — the eating, the exercise, all of it — because otherwise it feels like I’m not managing myself properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in restricting your food intake to avoid gaining weight or to feel more in control of your life?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly to avoid gaining weight, if I’m being completely honest. The control part is there, but it almost comes after. Like… the fear happens first, and then the controlling behavior is how I try to deal with the fear.\n\nIf the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic, like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It’s not just, “Oh, I gained a little,” or something neutral. It feels much bigger than that in my head. It feels like evidence that I’ve been careless, or that I’m slipping, or that I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing.\n\nSo when I restrict, it’s not really because I’m calmly trying to organize my life or feel generally in control. It’s more like I’m trying to prevent that panic from happening. I’m trying to keep the number from going up, because if it does, even slightly, I immediately feel like I have to correct it somehow. The control is wrapped into it, but the main goal is avoiding the weight gain.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more about preventing weight gain, I think. I mean, I know “control” is part of it, because when I keep to the routine I do feel like I’m holding things together in a way. But the thing that sets it off is the weight. That’s the part that feels non-negotiable.

If the scale goes up even a fraction, I get this really sharp panic — like immediately, before I’ve even had time to think about whether it could be water or timing or whatever. It feels like I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. And then I start going over everything in my head, what I ate, whether I was too lenient, whether I need to be stricter again.

So, yes, feeling in control matters, but it’s not some general life-control thing in a calm way. It’s more that preventing weight gain is what makes me feel like I haven’t failed at controlling myself. If the number moves up, even slightly, it feels like something is slipping, and I can’t just let that happen.
